#### Persistent Geopolitical Instability and Armed Conflicts  
Merck & Co., Inc. faces ongoing risks from geopolitical instability, particularly stemming from the Russo-Ukrainian War that began in early 2022. This conflict continues to disrupt global supply chains and increase commodity and logistics costs, affecting the pharmaceutical industry’s complex sourcing and distribution networks. Moreover, political unrest and conflicts in key regions can lead to interruptions in operations and increased compliance complexities. The protracted nature of such conflicts introduces uncertainty in international markets and can hinder the company’s ability to forecast and plan effectively.

#### Regulatory Uncertainty and Political Transitions in Core Markets  
Merck operates in a regulatory environment shaped by frequent political transitions and policy fluctuations, especially in the United States and Europe. Recent U.S. presidential elections and power transfers, including the highly polarized 2020 and 2016 cycles, have fueled uncertainty related to healthcare policy, drug pricing reforms, and regulatory frameworks. These changes can affect market access, reimbursement rates, and compliance costs. Similarly, developments like Brexit continue to reshape trade agreements and regulatory harmonization, potentially increasing operational complexity and administrative costs.

#### Exposure to Health Crises and Associated Societal Disruptions  
The company continues to be impacted by public health emergencies, most notably the COVID-19 pandemic and subsequent outbreaks such as monkeypox. These health crises disrupt the pharmaceutical operating environment through supply chain interruptions, shifts in healthcare demand, and delays in clinical development activities. Additionally, associated social unrest—like protests triggered during the COVID-19 pandemic—can affect local operations and workforce stability in affected regions. The unpredictability and recurrence of pandemics represent a persistent risk to operational continuity and revenue stability.

#### Increasing Frequency of Protests and Social Unrest  
Merck is exposed to operational risks posed by rising social unrest and protests across various geographies. Events such as the Charlottesville rally in the United States and COVID-19 related demonstrations illustrate the potential for disruption in markets critical to the company’s operations. These incidents can lead to temporary shutdowns, increased security costs, and reputational risks, complicating local market dynamics and potentially delaying product delivery and development timelines.

---

This assessment reflects the primary operating environment risks for Merck & Co., Inc., emphasizing external factors that could adversely affect the company’s credit profile through heightened uncertainty, operational disruption, and increased costs. The interplay of geopolitical tensions, regulatory fluidity, health crises, and social volatility underscores a challenging external landscape for the company in 2025.
# FINAL
---------------------------------------------------
# Key Rating Drivers – Merck & Co., Inc. (2025 Credit Risk Assessment)

The credit profile of Merck & Co., Inc. for 2025 reflects a combination of internal financial vulnerabilities, complex business dynamics, and a challenging external operating environment. While the company maintains strong liquidity and substantial cash reserves, notable negative credit factors stem from its earnings and cash flow volatility, financial leverage fluctuations, governance complexities, and material risks embedded in geopolitical and regulatory landscapes. The following sections analyze the most significant rating drivers, with a focus on potential downside risks to creditworthiness.

---

## Financial Profile (F1)

### Significant Volatility in EBITDA Margins and Profitability  
Merck’s historical EBITDA margins have exhibited substantial volatility, plunging to approximately 11.5% in 2023 before rebounding sharply to around 40% in 2024. This degree of margin fluctuation signals inconsistent profitability and raises concerns over the sustainability of stable earnings. Such swings may result from irregular revenue streams, fluctuating product dynamics, or variance in cost structures, which challenge reliable forecasting and heighten the risk to ongoing debt service capacity. Compared to sector peers like AbbVie—who have maintained margins ranging more consistently between 26% and 42%—Merck’s earnings instability is a material credit risk.

### Fluctuating and Elevated Leverage Levels  
The company’s leverage ratios reflect marked instability, with EBITDA leverage surging above 5.0x in 2023 before retreating to roughly 1.44x in 2024. This pronounced leverage spike underscores Merck’s vulnerability to earnings weakness or operational setbacks that could severely strain financial flexibility. Although current leverage levels appear moderate relative to cash generation, the historical volatility suggests that any future earnings decline could quickly erode debt capacity and diminish covenant headroom. Corresponding FFO leverage trends mirror this concern, underlying the criticality of consistent cash flow to maintain prudent credit metrics.

### Interest Coverage Margins Sensitive to Earnings Volatility  
Merck’s interest coverage ratio, based on funds from operations, stood near 3.27x in 2024, showing adequate present capacity to meet interest obligations. However, this metric is notably sensitive to earnings fluctuations—any 10–20% downturn in FFO or CFO could materially compress interest coverage, reducing the company’s ability to absorb shocks and secure debt service flexibility. This sensitivity amplifies financial risk amid Merck’s erratic profitability and operating cash flow patterns.

### Variability in Cash Flow Conversion and Free Cash Flow Consistency  
While Merck historically generates substantial cash flow from operations and free cash flow, conversion of EBITDA into operating cash flow has been uneven, particularly amid the EBITDA margin contraction in 2023. Differences in growth rates between EBITDA and CFO hint at potential challenges in working capital management or the impact of non-recurring cash flow items. This unpredictability constrains the reliability of cash flow forecasts and investor confidence in cash quality. Although the free cash flow ratio improved to about 0.49 in 2024, its inherent volatility limits the buffer available for debt reduction or discretionary investments.

### Limited Near-Term Liquidity Cushion Despite Strong Cash Position  
Holding approximately $13.2 billion in cash and a cash-to-short-term debt ratio near 5.0x reflects strong short-term liquidity. Nonetheless, the moderate free cash flow generation (ratio < 0.5) after capital expenditures indicates a limited cushion to absorb unforeseen cash needs or refinancing pressures. Combined with prior episodes of elevated leverage, this moderate free cash flow buffer exposes Merck to potential liquidity stress if operational performance deteriorates or capital markets conditions tighten.

### Elevated Exposure to Earnings and Cash Flow Volatility Heightens Credit Risk  
The pronounced variability in profitability, leverage, and cash flow metrics over recent years substantially increases credit risk. Earnings volatility not only pressures leverage multiples and coverage ratios but also narrows financial flexibility, heightening refinancing risks and potentially increasing funding costs in downturn scenarios. Without a sustained improvement in earnings stability and cash flow quality, Merck faces a continued risk of credit metric deterioration.

### CapEx Levels Constrain Free Cash Flow Upside  
Capital expenditures remain consistently high, ranging between $3.4 billion and $4.4 billion annually. While steady CapEx reflects ongoing investment needs, the relatively elevated and stable spending limits the company’s ability to expand free cash flow, especially during periods of earnings compression. This dynamic restricts discretionary liquidity available for debt reduction or strategic initiatives, intensifying sensitivity to operational shocks.

### Weaker Financial Stability Relative to Key Peer AbbVie  
Merck exhibits considerably greater earnings and cash flow volatility than AbbVie, which maintains more stable margins and superior liquidity flexibility. The comparative inconsistency in both leverage and cash flow conversion metrics underscores Merck’s comparatively heightened credit risk, mandating close monitoring of cash flow quality, margin sustainability, and leverage management.

---

## Business Profile (F2)

### Elevated Insider Stock Awards Increasing Dilution Risk  
Merck continues to rely heavily on phantom stock awards for executive compensation in 2025, which could erode shareholder equity over time. The persistent use of equity-based incentives highlights management’s dependence on stock grants to retain key personnel but increases potential dilution and may weigh negatively on earnings per share and investor sentiment. This dilution risk may indirectly impact financial flexibility by affecting equity market valuation and cost of capital.

### Absence of Insider Buying Weakens Positive Confidence Signals  
Insider transactions remain dominated by phantom stock awards, with negligible stock purchases by executives. The lack of insider buying removes an important signal of management conviction in the company’s near-term valuation and growth prospects. In an industry facing regulatory uncertainty and volatile pipeline outcomes, this absence of positive insider market activity may raise concerns among investors about management’s confidence and alignment with shareholders.

### Complex Ownership Structure via Minority Stakes in Multiple Biotech Affiliates  
Merck holds approximately 10% minority interests in several biotech entities such as Organon & Co., Harpoon Therapeutics, and BeOne Medicines. While offering access to innovative pipelines, limited ownership stakes restrict direct operational control and complicate governance. The complexities arising from multiple partially controlled ventures introduce strategic alignment risks and may result in uneven financial and operational performance, creating incremental uncertainty in Merck’s overall business profile.

### Lack of Insider Buying Further Detracts From Management-Shareholder Alignment  
The near absence of insider stock acquisitions in 2025 diminishes positive market signals regarding management’s confidence. In the context of pharmaceutical sector challenges—including regulatory reforms and clinical development risk—strong insider buying often denotes alignment with shareholder interests. Its absence potentially heightens perceived risks around strategic execution and long-term growth potential.

---

## Operating Environment (F3)

### Persistent Geopolitical Instability and Supply Chain Disruptions  
The ongoing Russo-Ukrainian War and assorted global conflicts continue to destabilize supply chains critical to Merck’s operations. Increased commodity prices, elevated logistics costs, and disruptions in sourcing expose the company to operational interruptions and inflationary pressures. Such geopolitical risks limit visibility into global market conditions and constrain strategic planning, representing a persistent external credit risk factor.

### Regulatory Uncertainty and Political Transitions in Core Markets  
Merck faces regulatory volatility driven by political transitions, particularly in the United States and Europe. Unpredictability around healthcare policies, drug pricing reforms, and evolving regulatory frameworks can materially affect reimbursement rates, market access, and compliance expenses. The regulatory fragmentation stemming from events like Brexit compounds administrative complexity and cost structures, posing ongoing challenges to stable operating performance.

### Exposure to Health Crises and Their Operational Aftershocks  
Recurring health emergencies, including the COVID-19 pandemic and monkeypox outbreaks, continue to disrupt pharmaceutical supply chains, demand patterns, and clinical development timelines. Additionally, associated social disruptions and protests in affected regions may interrupt operations and workforce stability. The persistent threat of pandemics creates an uncertain environment, potentially affecting revenue continuity and operational resilience.

### Increasing Frequency of Social Unrest and Protest Risks  
Merck’s operations are exposed to market disruptions arising from social unrest, manifesting in protests such as those related to COVID-19 policies or civil rights demonstrations in the U.S. These events can incur elevated security costs, temporary operational shutdowns, reputational risks, and delays in product delivery schedules. The rising social volatility complicates local market conditions, imposing additional risk layers on operational continuity and cost management.

---

## Conclusion

Merck & Co., Inc.’s 2025 credit profile is characterized by significant financial volatility and operational uncertainties that elevate credit risk despite strong liquidity reserves. Pronounced earnings and EBITDA margin fluctuations, coupled with leverage spikes and sensitive coverage ratios, expose the company to financial instability in adverse scenarios. Compounding these concerns, complex ownership arrangements and a lack of positive insider buying diminish confidence in management alignment and strategic clarity. Externally, a turbulent geopolitical landscape, regulatory unpredictability, and recurrent health crises impose challenging operating conditions with potential to disrupt cash flow and operational stability.

Moving forward, mitigating these risks will require Merck to stabilize earnings and cash flow generation, maintain prudent leverage levels, and navigate complex political and regulatory environments effectively. The credit profile remains sensitive to downside shocks related to revenue consistency, refinancing flexibility, and external disruptions, warranting close monitoring by stakeholders throughout 2025.
# FINAL - with sample
---------------------------------------------------
### Key Rating Drivers – Merck & Co., Inc. (2025)

Merck & Co., Inc.’s credit profile in 2025 is shaped by a combination of internal financial challenges, complex business dynamics, and a demanding operating environment. The following key rating drivers focus primarily on the primary risks and weaknesses influencing the company’s credit risk, alongside interrelated factors from its financial, business, and operating environment profiles.

---

#### Financial Profile (F1)

**Significant Earnings and EBITDA Margin Volatility Undermining Profit Stability**  
Merck has experienced pronounced swings in EBITDA margins and profitability in recent years, with a steep dip to roughly 11.5% in 2023 followed by a rebound to nearly 40% in 2024. This wide variation underscores unstable earnings generation and reduces predictability for debt servicing and operational funding. Such volatility largely reflects uneven revenue streams and fluctuating cost structures, complicating financial planning and increasing downside risks to cash flow in adverse scenarios. Compared to industry peers such as AbbVie, which demonstrates more consistent margin performance, Merck’s earnings instability stands out as a notable credit weakness.

**Fluctuating and Historically Elevated Leverage Heightens Financial Risk**  
Leverage metrics have mirrored the earnings volatility, with EBITDA multiple spiking above 5.0x in 2023 before improving to about 1.44x in 2024. This wide range indicates heightened risk during weaker earnings periods, when debt servicing pressure intensifies and refinancing flexibility may shrink. The FFO-based leverage trend aligns with this pattern, pointing to Merck’s dependence on robust and stable cash flow to maintain comfortable credit metrics. While current leverage levels appear moderate, the historical swings diminish confidence in sustained financial stability if market or operational headwinds worsen.

**Interest Coverage Is Currently Adequate but Sensitive to Earnings Downturns**  
With interest coverage around 3.27x in 2024, Merck demonstrates a sufficient but relatively slim buffer for meeting debt servicing obligations. Given the company’s history of earnings fluctuations, even moderate declines of 10-20% in funds from operations could quickly erode coverage ratios, heightening refinancing and liquidity risks. This sensitivity reflects underlying earnings and cash flow volatility and constrains the company’s financial flexibility in stress scenarios.

**Variable Cash Flow Conversion and Cash Flow Quality Limit Predictability**  
Despite generating strong absolute cash flow amounts, the conversion of EBITDA into operating cash flow and free cash flow has been inconsistent, notably affected by the 2023 performance trough. Such variability, likely stemming from working capital management challenges or non-recurring cash effects, reduces confidence in the reliability of free cash flow for reinvestment or debt reduction. Although free cash flow ratios improved substantially in 2024, the prior swings restrict effective liquidity planning and undermine credit stability, especially considering ongoing sizeable capital expenditures.

**Limited Free Cash Flow Cushion Post-CapEx Increases Operational Vulnerability**  
Capital expenditures of approximately $3.4 billion to $4.4 billion annually, while stable, impose a meaningful drag on free cash flow generation, particularly in years when EBITDA margins compress sharply. This consistent investment level reduces headroom to rapidly deleverage or expand discretionary cash flow, increasing Merck’s exposure to refinancing risk and financial strain during earnings downturns. The restricted free cash flow cushion is an important constraint on financial flexibility, emphasizing the company’s dependence on maintaining stable operating profitability.

**Weaker Financial Stability Compared to Sector Peers Elevates Relative Credit Risk**  
When benchmarked against peers such as AbbVie, Merck’s credit profile appears more vulnerable due to greater earnings and cash flow volatility, inconsistent margin trends, and tighter free cash flow ratios. AbbVie’s steadier financial metrics and more stable liquidity position offer comparative resilience, highlighting Merck’s need for close monitoring of earnings quality and debt capacity as key mitigants against heightened credit risk.

---

#### Business Profile (F2)

**Elevated Dilution Risk from Substantial Insider Stock Award Program**  
In 2025, Merck continued a significant issuance of phantom stock awards to executives and insiders as a core component of compensation. While prevalent in the pharmaceutical industry, the ongoing reliance on equity-based incentives increases the risk of shareholder dilution, potentially pressuring earnings per share and diminishing investor returns over time. This dilution factor introduces an additional layer of financial risk by affecting equity stability and potentially increasing cost of capital.

**Management Incentive Alignment and Confidence Are Constrained by Insider Transaction Patterns**  
The bulk of insider transactions in 2025 involved equity awards rather than stock purchases, signaling a lack of insider buying. This cautious stance from management limits positive market perception of internal confidence in Merck’s valuation and growth outlook. In an industry faced with regulatory and pipeline risks, insider buying often serves as a confidence indicator; its absence increases concerns regarding management’s conviction in near-term prospects and tightens alignment with shareholder interests.

**Operational and Governance Complexity Due to Minority Stakes in Multiple Biotech Entities**  
Merck’s broad portfolio of minority holdings (around 10%) in various biotech and pharmaceutical affiliates—including Organon & Co., Harpoon Therapeutics, and BeOne Medicines—adds organizational complexity. Limited ownership restricts direct control over strategic decisions and operational alignment, which complicates coordination, increases governance risks, and exposes Merck to uneven financial and operational performance outcomes from these affiliates. This dispersed ownership model introduces uncertainty and potential volatility beyond the core business operations.

---

#### Operating Environment (F3)

**Persistent Geopolitical Instability and Global Conflict-Driven Supply Disruptions**  
The ongoing Russo-Ukrainian War and related geopolitical tensions continue to disrupt global pharmaceutical supply chains, elevate commodity prices, and increase logistics costs. Merck faces challenges in sourcing critical inputs and delivering products efficiently, with potential operational interruptions and increased compliance burdens in affected regions. The protracted conflict environment injects sustained uncertainty into international markets, complicating Merck’s forecasting and operational continuity efforts.

**Regulatory and Political Uncertainty in Core Markets Adds Operational Complexity**  
Merck operates amid shifting regulatory landscapes influenced by political transitions, especially in the United States and Europe. Drug pricing reforms, healthcare policy changes, and evolving regulatory frameworks create uncertain market access and reimbursement conditions. Additionally, outcomes from events like Brexit introduce trade complexities and potential increases in administrative costs. This regulatory fluidity elevates compliance risks and can adversely impact revenue stability and cost management.

**Vulnerability to Health Crises and Social Disruptions Weigh on Operational Stability**  
Periodic public health emergencies such as COVID-19 and subsequent outbreaks have imposed supply chain interruptions, changed healthcare demand patterns, and delayed clinical development timelines. These dynamics persist as risks, compounding operational challenges. Furthermore, related social unrest and protests increase the risk of workforce instability, temporary disruptions, and added security costs in key markets, complicating operational execution and potentially impacting reputation.

**Increasing Social Unrest and Protests Amplify Operational Risks**  
Escalating social unrest events, exemplified by incidents like the Charlottesville rally and pandemic-related demonstrations, disrupt markets critical to Merck’s operations. Temporary market shutdowns, heightened security expenditures, and reputational impacts arising from these incidents impose additional operational and financial burdens. Such volatility adds an external layer of risk to the company’s ability to maintain steady market presence and operational efficiency.

---

### Conclusion

Merck & Co., Inc.’s credit profile in 2025 is principally challenged by sizable earnings and cash flow volatility, fluctuating leverage, and a constrained free cash flow buffer, which collectively heighten refinancing and operational risks. These financial vulnerabilities are compounded by management’s reliance on equity compensation, limited insider confidence signals, and organizational complexity from minority affiliate stakes. Externally, a volatile geopolitical landscape, regulatory uncertainty, and recurring health crises further increase the risk environment. Mitigating these interlinked factors will require ongoing improvements in earnings consistency, cash flow quality, and strategic alignment to preserve financial flexibility and sustain creditworthiness.
# V0
---------------------------------------------------
### Key Rating Drivers for Occidental Petroleum Corp (CIK 0000797468) – 2023

---

#### F1. Elevated Leverage and Debt Burden  
Occidental Petroleum continues to operate with a notably high leverage profile, driven by sustained debt accumulation from past acquisitions and capital-intensive operational requirements. Despite some deleveraging efforts, the company’s debt-to-EBITDA ratio remains elevated relative to industry peers, constraining financial flexibility. This high leverage increases vulnerability to interest rate fluctuations and limits Occidental’s ability to absorb operational disruptions or invest prudently in growth initiatives. Coupled with rising interest expenses amid a challenging monetary environment, the debt service burden creates downside risk to credit metrics, particularly if commodity price volatility pressures cash flow generation.

---

#### F1. Volatile Cash Flows Impacted by Commodity Price Exposure  
Occidental’s financial results are inherently tied to fluctuations in oil and gas prices, which remain volatile given geopolitical tensions, global energy demand uncertainties, and evolving energy transition policies. The company’s cash flow volatility elevates credit risk by complicating forecasting and potentially necessitating opportunistic balance sheet management. This sensitivity amplifies the challenges in maintaining stable credit metrics and fund capital programs while meeting debt obligations under adverse market conditions.

---

#### F2. Strategic and Operational Risks from Aggressive Growth Strategy  
While Occidental has sought growth through acquisitions and expansion of its upstream portfolio, including enhanced oil recovery projects, this strategy carries execution risk and integration challenges. The reliance on technologically complex and capital-intensive projects exposes the company to operational setbacks and cost overruns, which can negatively affect profitability and cash flow. Additionally, strategic decisions to maintain sizable investments in carbon capture and other emerging technologies, although potentially beneficial long-term, weigh on near-term free cash flow and financial flexibility.

---

#### F2. Corporate Governance and Ownership Concentration  
Occidental’s governance framework reflects significant influence from its largest shareholders, particularly activist investors and private equity partners. Concentrated ownership could lead to strategic shifts prioritizing short- to medium-term financial metrics at the expense of longer-term credit stability. Furthermore, potential conflicts may arise between minority shareholders and controlling stakeholders, complicating decision-making in periods of financial stress and creating uncertainty in capital allocation policies.

---

#### F3. Challenging and Uncertain Operating Environment  
The external environment for Occidental remains fraught with risks stemming from regulatory changes, particularly tightening environmental policies and escalating carbon pricing regimes across major markets. Increased compliance costs and potential restrictions on emissions-heavy operations add uncertainty and operational costs. Moreover, global economic growth concerns amid inflationary pressures and shifting energy demand patterns pose risks to oil price recovery, thereby impacting revenue streams and credit metrics.

---

#### F3. Sectoral Transition and Market Sentiment Risks  
Occidental operates within a sector undergoing significant transformation driven by the global energy transition towards lower-carbon alternatives. This transition exerts pressure on market valuation and increases the cost of capital for traditional hydrocarbons producers. Investor and lender sentiment is increasingly wary of environmental, social, and governance (ESG) risks, which could limit Occidental’s access to favorable financing terms and increase refinancing risks over the medium term.

---

### Summary  
Occidental Petroleum’s credit profile is principally challenged by its elevated leverage and volatile cash flows rooted in commodity price exposure. These financial pressures are compounded by strategic risks in its growth projects and governance complexities. The macroeconomic and sector-specific transition risks further weigh on its credit standing, creating an environment of uncertainty that undermines financial resilience and flexibility going forward.

### Key Rating Drivers – Financial Profile (F1)

**Declining Profitability and EBITDA Margin Pressure**  
Occidental Petroleum’s EBITDA margin has weakened from 60.3% in 2022 to approximately 50.4% in 2023, signaling a notable erosion in profitability. While the margin remains above 50%, this downward trend raises concerns about the company’s ability to sustain high earnings relative to revenue. Compared to Diamondback Energy, which maintained significantly higher EBITDA margins above 73% in 2023, Occidental’s profitability is comparatively weaker, which may constrain its capacity to generate sufficient cash flows for debt servicing, especially during periods of commodity price volatility.

**Decreased Cash Flow Conversion and Cash Generation Volatility**  
Although Occidental continues to generate substantial cash flow, there is evidence of deteriorating efficiency in translating EBITDA into free cash flow. From 2022 to 2023, the free cash flow ratio declined substantially to approximately 0.29 from near 0.59, indicating that a smaller proportion of earnings are being converted into discretionary cash after capital expenditures. The marked increase in capital spending to over $6.2 billion further strains cash flow generation relative to prior years. While absolute free cash flow remains positive, the declining ratio signals reduced financial flexibility and potential vulnerability to cash flow shocks.

**Moderate but Rising Leverage with Limited Cushion**  
Leverage metrics have trended upward in 2023, with EBITDA leverage increasing to approximately 1.47x and FFO leverage to 1.36x, compared to materially lower levels the previous year. Although current leverage appears moderate and manageable, the rising trajectory combined with declining cash flow conversion suggests less room for additional debt accumulation and heightened refinancing risk. This contrasts with Diamondback Energy, which exhibits lower FFO leverage ratios under 1.0x, reflecting Occidental’s relatively more leveraged position within the peer group.

**Solid but Potentially Vulnerable Interest Coverage Ratios**  
The company maintains a healthy FFO interest coverage ratio above 6x, which currently indicates comfortable earnings cushion over interest expenses. However, given the margin erosion and rising leverage, these coverage ratios could deteriorate quickly under scenarios of a 10-20% decline in cash flows, reducing the buffer against earnings volatility. This sensitivity to down-side earnings pressures exposes Occidental to increased credit risk, especially if commodity prices weaken or operating costs rise unexpectedly.

**Liquidity Position Showing Signs of Tightening**  
Although total cash balances improved modestly to about $1.4 billion in 2023, the ratio of cash to short-term debt remains low at roughly 1.35x, a significant decline from exceptionally strong levels seen in 2022. This tighter liquidity position, combined with sizeable short-term obligations, could test Occidental’s near-term funding flexibility. Additionally, the free cash flow ratio below 0.3 suggests limited free cash cushioning after capital expenditures, intensifying potential liquidity stresses if operating cash flow declines.

**Elevated Capital Expenditures Straining Free Cash Flow**  
Occidental’s capital spending surged to more than $6.2 billion in 2023, representing a substantial increase relative to previous years and a significant drain on free cash flow generation. This heavy investment focus may reflect strategic growth or maintenance needs but simultaneously reduces available cash for debt repayment or liquidity reserves. Persistent high CapEx levels relative to EBITDA could dampen financial flexibility over the medium term, particularly in a lower commodity price environment.

---

In summary, Occidental Petroleum's financial profile in 2023 is characterized by weakening profitability and cash flow conversion, moderately increasing leverage, and tightening liquidity metrics. Although current interest coverage ratios remain adequate, declining margins and elevated capital expenditures introduce risk to the company’s capacity to comfortably meet debt obligations. Compared with peers, Occidental exhibits relatively lower profitability and higher leverage, underscoring the need for prudent financial management to maintain credit stability amid volatile market conditions.
# V1 corrected
---------------------------------------------------
### Key Rating Drivers – Financial Profile (F1)

**Erosion of EBITDA Margin Undermines Profitability**  
Occidental Petroleum’s EBITDA margin declined significantly from 60.3% in 2022 to approximately 50.4% in 2023. This margin compression points to deteriorating profitability amid a challenging operating backdrop. While the company remains profitable on an absolute basis, the decline erodes its earnings buffer and constrains its ability to generate cash to support ongoing debt servicing and capital investment. Compared to Diamondback Energy’s premium margin near 74%, Occidental’s lower profitability accentuates competitive pressure within the sector.

**Deteriorating Free Cash Flow Conversion Despite High EBITDA**  
Although EBITDA remains robust at $14.2 billion, the company’s efficiency in converting earnings into free cash flow has weakened considerably. The free cash flow ratio dropped to 0.29 in 2023 from 0.59 in 2022 as capital expenditures surged to $6.27 billion from $4.5 billion. This substantial increase in CapEx significantly reduced net cash generation after investments. While operational cash flow remains strong, the growing capex burden limits discretionary cash available for debt repayment and adds strain to financial flexibility.

**Rising Leverage with Moderate Cushion**  
Leverage metrics reflect a modest increase over the past year, with EBITDA leverage rising to approximately 1.47x and FFO leverage to 1.36x at the end of 2023. Though these levels are still within moderate ranges, the upward trend combined with reduced cash flow conversion signals a tightening financial structure. Relative to peers like Diamondback Energy, which shows lower FFO leverage under 1.0x, Occidental’s debt burden is more elevated, increasing vulnerability should cash flows weaken or debt refinancing conditions tighten.

**Interest Coverage Remains Adequate but Sensitive to Earnings Volatility**  
The company’s FFO interest coverage of 6.26x indicates a currently comfortable buffer between cash earnings and interest expense. However, this coverage is sensitive to declines in cash flows given margin erosion and increased financial leverage. A moderate 10-20% drop in FFO could materially narrow coverage, reducing the company’s ability to absorb earnings shocks and increasing credit risk amid more challenging market conditions.

**Liquidity Position Shows Signs of Contraction**  
Occidental’s liquidity, measured by cash relative to short-term debt, tightened to a ratio of approximately 1.35x in 2023, down sharply from the exceptionally strong levels seen in 2022. While absolute cash balances increased slightly, the significant rise in short-term liabilities reduces near-term financial flexibility. Coupled with a free cash flow ratio below 0.3, this constrains the company’s capacity to manage immediate obligations and increases refinancing risk in a potentially volatile market environment.

**Elevated Capital Expenditures Limit Financial Flexibility**  
The substantial increase in capital expenditures to $6.27 billion in 2023, up nearly 40% year-over-year, represents a significant drain on cash resources. This elevated investment level weighs on free cash flow generation and restricts Occidental’s ability to deleverage or build liquidity buffers. Continued high capital intensity may heighten credit risk if operating cash flows were to soften, given the limited free cash flow available for other uses.

**Substantial Volatility in Cash Flow Metrics Despite Strong EBITDA**  
While EBITDA and Funds From Operations (FFO) remain at strong absolute levels, discrepancies between EBITDA, CFO, and FCF growth highlight volatility in cash flow quality. Free cash flow declined markedly despite stable underlying cash from operations, illustrating how cash generation is increasingly sensitive to capital spending decisions. Such variability in cash quality may challenge cash flow predictability and financial planning under market stress.

---

Overall, Occidental Petroleum’s 2023 financial profile reflects increased risk stemming from margin compression, rising leverage, constrained liquidity, and heavy capital spending. Although coverage ratios and cash flow remain sufficient at present, the intensifying capital expenditure program and tightening liquidity raise concerns about the company’s ability to preserve financial flexibility. Relative to sector peers, Occidental faces heightened pressure on profitability and financial structure, underscoring the need for ongoing cash flow discipline and prudent debt management to maintain creditworthiness.

### Key Rating Drivers – Business Profile (F2)

**1. Elevated Insider Trading Activity by Major Shareholders Suggests Potential Strategic Repositioning**  
Throughout 2023, Occidental Petroleum Corp. experienced relatively high volumes of insider share transactions, particularly involving Berkshire Hathaway Inc. and its principal Warren Buffett. The concentrated pattern of purchases at year-end reflects a deliberate bolstering of stakes by a major institutional shareholder, which may signal shifts in strategic views or confidence levels yet also introduces uncertainty regarding management’s future direction. Such concentrated activity, amid limited transactions by other insiders, could indicate potential re-alignment of control or influence that may impact strategic decision-making agility.

**2. Concentration of Ownership with Limited Insider Diversification Raises Governance Concerns**  
Ownership remains substantially concentrated among a few significant shareholders, primarily Berkshire Hathaway, without broadening of insider stakeholder diversity. While concentrated ownership may foster decisiveness, it poses risks related to potential conflicts of interest and limited governance checks and balances. The relative inactivity and disposition of stock by other executives or board members highlight a potential lack of unified strategic vision or alignment among the broader management team and controlling investors, which could impair optimal decision-making in volatile market conditions.

**3. Increasing Exposure to Affiliates and Complex Subsidiary Structure Adds Strategic Complexity**  
Occidental’s extensive ownership stakes in multiple entities—such as Premcor Inc., California Resources Corp, Plains GP Holdings LP, Plains All American Pipeline LP, and NET Power Inc.—create a multi-layered corporate group structure. While diversification across energy-related businesses can be positive, this complexity raises risks around capital allocation, concentration of operational risk, and potential transfer of resources to weaker subsidiaries. The varying ownership stakes and differing strategic priorities within this group may dilute management focus, reduce operational synergies, and constrain flexibility to respond swiftly to market changes, thereby increasing credit risk.

**4. Limited Insider Purchases Beyond Major Shareholders Indicates Uncertain Internal Confidence**  
There is relatively sparse share acquisition activity by executives other than the dominant investors, and some documented insider sales during the year. Such selective participation may potentially reflect cautious or divergent views on the company’s medium- to long-term prospects from management ranks. This uneven insider engagement in ownership could suggest a level of uncertainty or lack of conviction in the current strategic path, which may translate into frictions in strategy execution or inconsistent commitment to credit stability objectives.

**5. Absence of Significant Stock-Based Compensation-Related Dilution Suggests Low Near-Term Earnings Dilution Risk but Also Potentially Limited Management Incentive Alignment**  
The data shows minimal issuance of stock awards or options to insiders during 2023. While this limits the immediate risk of equity dilution—a positive from a credit risk perspective—it also raises questions about the effectiveness of compensation structures in motivating management toward sustained performance improvements. In an industry characterized by cyclical volatility, inadequate shareholder-aligned incentives can impair long-term strategic focus and operational discipline, constituting a subtle governance weakness affecting credit quality.

---

Overall, Occidental Petroleum’s business profile reflects a company grappling with structural complexity and concentrated ownership dynamics that pose notable challenges to governance cohesion and strategic clarity. These factors heighten execution risks that may adversely influence credit stability in a demanding operating environment.
# V2 corrected
---------------------------------------------------
### Key Rating Drivers – Business Profile (F2)

**1. Significant Insider Purchasing by Berkshire Hathaway Reflects Concentrated Influence and Potential Strategic Shifts**  
In 2023, Berkshire Hathaway Inc. and Warren Buffett substantially increased their shareholdings in Occidental Petroleum, with multiple large purchases recorded, especially towards year-end. This significant insider activity by a dominant stakeholder signals a potentially evolving strategic direction driven by a principal investor rather than broad-based internal management consensus. While this can provide stability, it also concentrates decision-making influence, which may limit diverse strategic perspectives and increase dependence on a single shareholder’s priorities.

**2. Concentrated Ownership Structure May Limit Governance Transparency and Strategic Flexibility**  
The company’s ownership remains highly concentrated, primarily controlled by a few large shareholders such as Berkshire Hathaway, with limited diversification among other insiders or management. Such concentration reduces the diversity of oversight and may weaken governance dynamics, potentially affecting the alignment of interests between minority shareholders and controlling parties. This ownership concentration carries the risk of decisions driven by a narrow set of stakeholders, which may not always align with optimizing credit risk outcomes.

**3. Complex and Expansive Subsidiary and Affiliate Holdings Add Operational and Strategic Risk**  
Occidental holds significant ownership stakes in various affiliates, including Premcor Inc., California Resources Corp, Plains GP Holdings LP, Plains All American Pipeline LP, NET Power Inc., and Western Midstream Partners. The broad and complex group structure increases management challenges related to capital allocation, corporate governance, and operational integration across diverse energy segments. This complexity raises the risk that weaker subsidiaries or affiliates could strain resources or divert attention from core operations, adversely impacting the company’s overall credit profile.

**4. Limited Insider Activity Outside of Major Shareholders Suggests Potentially Mixed Internal Confidence**  
Insider transactions reveal limited purchasing activity by executives beyond the dominant shareholder group, with some disposition of shares noted among other insiders. This uneven participation may indicate a lack of unanimous confidence regarding the company’s strategic outlook or medium-term performance prospects within the broader management team. Such cautious insider behavior could signal internal uncertainty, potentially undermining unified execution of strategic initiatives critical to credit stability.

**5. Minimal New Stock-Based Compensation Transactions Indicate Low Immediate Dilution but Potential Governance Concerns**  
No material issuance of stock options, awards, or other insider compensation-related equity transactions was observed in 2023. While this reduces near-term dilution risk, it may also reflect a compensation structure that is less focused on shareholder-value alignment. In an industry marked by volatility, absence of robust equity incentives might hamper management’s motivation to pursue disciplined capital allocation and operational excellence, indirectly posing longer-term risks to strategic consistency and credit strength.

---

Occidental Petroleum’s business profile is characterized by concentrated ownership with evolving dominance of a key institutional investor, combined with a complex network of subsidiaries and affiliated entities. These factors introduce governance and strategic execution challenges that elevate credit risk, particularly in a capital-intensive and cyclical industry environment.

### Key Rating Drivers: Operating Environment (F3)

#### Elevated Geopolitical Risks from International Conflicts and Wars  
Occidental Petroleum operates within a sector highly exposed to geopolitical instability and conflicts, which have intensified over the past decade and continue to pose substantial risks. Most notably, the ongoing Russo-Ukrainian War, which escalated in 2022, has disrupted global energy markets and introduced volatility in supply chains. This conflict has led to supply uncertainties, fluctuating commodity prices, and increased costs for energy companies, including Occidental. The heightened state of international tension reduces predictability in operations, potentially affecting capital expenditure plans and asset valuations. Persistent geopolitical conflicts also increase risks related to sanctions and trade restrictions, which could curtail Occidental’s access to key markets or limit operational flexibility in affected regions.

#### Exposure to Domestic Social Unrest and Political Volatility in the United States  
Recent years have witnessed significant social and political unrest in the United States, including widespread protests triggered by ethnic and religious tensions in 2020, as well as contested electoral processes. Occidental has been identified as impacted by these societal upheavals, which introduce operational challenges such as disruptions to infrastructure, increased security costs, and potential community opposition. Political volatility can also lead to abrupt policy shifts, affecting regulatory environments critical to fossil fuel producers. This environment constrains strategic planning and may weigh on investor confidence regarding mid-term stability.

#### Increasing Regulatory Pressure from Global Climate Agreements  
Global efforts to combat climate change, as exemplified by Occidental’s exposure to major international environmental agreements such as the Paris Agreement and commitment pledges emerging from COP28 in 2023, constitute a material operational risk. These accords drive tightening restrictions on carbon emissions and fossil fuel usage, compelling Occidental to adapt quickly to evolving regulatory frameworks. Compliance costs, potential carbon pricing mechanisms, and growing pressure from stakeholders to reduce environmental footprints could impair profitability and limit growth opportunities unless the company accelerates its transition strategies. The ongoing global pivot away from hydrocarbons increases uncertainty over the long-term demand outlook for crude petroleum and natural gas.

#### Vulnerability to Natural Disasters and Environmental Events  
Occidental’s operational footprint is susceptible to severe natural disasters, impacting physical assets and supply chains. Historical events such as Hurricane Katrina (2005), the largest oil spill in the Gulf of Mexico (2010), and more recently, Hurricane Ian (2022) demonstrate the company’s exposure to catastrophic weather incidents. These events pose direct financial risks through asset damage, operational shutdowns, increased insurance costs, and potential litigation or regulatory penalties. Climate change is likely to exacerbate the frequency and intensity of such disasters, magnifying the company’s exposure and challenging its capacity to maintain uninterrupted production and cash flow stability.

#### Geopolitical Instability in the Arab Region and Middle East   
Although Occidental’s primary operations are concentrated in the Americas, the broader crude petroleum sector remains vulnerable to unrest in key oil-producing regions, including the Arab countries. Historical revolutions and uprisings in Tunisia, Egypt, Libya, Yemen, and Bahrain have demonstrated how political turbulence can impact regional supply security and market volatility. The persistence of ethnic, religious, and political tensions in these areas underscores ongoing risks of production disruptions or supply shocks that could reverberate across global markets, indirectly pressuring Occidental’s operational environment and credit profile through commodity price volatility or supply uncertainty.

---

Overall, Occidental Petroleum’s credit risk profile is weighed down by significant operating environment challenges. These include persistent geopolitical conflicts, domestic social volatility, increasingly stringent environmental regulations, and physical risks from natural disasters. While the company benefits from longstanding sector expertise, these external risks require vigilant management to preserve financial and operational resilience.

# FINAL
---------------------------------------------------
# Key Rating Drivers – Occidental Petroleum Corp (2023)

The credit risk profile of Occidental Petroleum Corp in 2023 is shaped predominantly by a constellation of emerging financial pressures, complex business governance challenges, and a volatile operating environment. While the company’s absolute scale and cash flow generation remain substantial, multiple headwinds undermine its financial flexibility and strategic stability. These challenges, particularly concentrated in margin erosion, rising leverage, concentrated ownership, and external geopolitical and regulatory risks, elevate the inherent credit risk. Below is a detailed presentation of the principal rating drivers organized by financial profile, business profile, and operating environment factors.

---

## Financial Profile (F1)

### Erosion of EBITDA Margin Undermines Profitability and Cash Flow Cushion  
Occidental’s EBITDA margin contracted markedly from 60.3% in 2022 to roughly 50.4% in 2023. This steep margin compression signals weakening operational profitability amid an increasingly challenging commodity price environment and cost pressures. Although EBITDA remains strong at $14.2 billion, the diminished margin erodes Occidental’s critical earnings buffer, reducing its ability to generate discretionary cash flow for debt servicing and capital investment flexibility. This diminished profitability also widens the performance gap versus peers such as Diamondback Energy, whose higher margin profile near 74% suggests superior competitive positioning.

### Rising Capital Expenditures Intensify Strain on Free Cash Flow and Financial Flexibility  
Capital expenditures surged nearly 40% year-over-year to $6.27 billion, substantially outpacing growth in operating cash flow. The free cash flow conversion ratio declined sharply to 0.29 in 2023 from 0.59 in 2022, indicating that the company’s ability to translate EBITDA into real liquidity has weakened considerably. This elevated capex burden, amid volatile cash flow quality, limits Occidental’s capacity to deleverage or accumulate liquidity reserves. The heavy investment commitment raises important concerns over the sustainability of capital spending if market or commodity fundamentals deteriorate, thereby exacerbating credit risk.

### Upward Trending Leverage Narrows Cushion Amid Earning Volatility  
Despite currently moderate leverage ratios — EBITDA leverage at approximately 1.47x and FFO leverage at 1.36x — the upward trajectory compared to prior years compounds financial vulnerability. When coupled with the deteriorating cash flow conversion and margin erosion, these trends suggest tightening financial headroom. Notably, Occidental’s leverage remains elevated relative to some lower-levered industry peers, creating heightened sensitivity to potential cash flow shocks. Interest coverage, while at an adequate 6.26x on an FFO basis, is susceptible to rapid deterioration from relatively moderate earnings declines (10-20%), which would constrict Occidental’s debt service capacity.

### Liquidity Position Shows Signs of Contraction Increasing Refinancing Risk  
Cash relative to short-term debt has slipped to a ratio of approximately 1.35x in 2023 from previously robust levels. While absolute liquidity amounts edged up slightly, the sharp increase in near-term liabilities reduces Occidental’s immediate financial flexibility in a potentially volatile funding market. The combination of tighter liquidity and lower free cash flow generation intensifies refinancing risk, especially if credit markets weaken or interest rates rise, heightening the credit profile’s sensitivity to external shocks.

### Volatile Cash Flow Quality Undermines Predictability  
Notwithstanding strong EBITDA and FFO, the company exhibits meaningful volatility and divergence between reported EBITDA, operating cash flow, and free cash flow growth. The noticeable decline in free cash flow despite stable underlying cash from operations underscores a rising sensitivity of cash generation to capital allocation decisions. This variability complicates financial planning and risk mitigation, introducing an added layer of uncertainty into Occidental’s ability to maintain consistent debt coverage and investment programs during market stresses.

---

## Business Profile (F2)

### Concentrated Ownership by Berkshire Hathaway Creates Strategic Concentration Risk  
Berkshire Hathaway’s aggressive share accumulation throughout 2023, especially in the year’s latter half, signifies a concentration of governance and strategic influence in a single dominant investor. While this involvement could provide long-term stability, it simultaneously centralizes decision-making and heightens dependence on the priorities and risk tolerance of one controlling shareholder. Such concentration may curtail the diversity of strategic perspectives necessary in a capital-intensive, cyclical energy sector, potentially constraining agile responses to fluctuating market conditions and credit risk challenges.

### Complex Subsidiary and Affiliate Structure Heightens Operational and Strategic Risk  
Occidental’s substantial holdings across a wide network of affiliates — including Premcor Inc., California Resources Corp, Plains GP Holdings, NET Power, and others — adds significant complexity to corporate governance and capital allocation. The broad asset and operational footprint necessitates sophisticated management oversight, with weaker or underperforming entities potentially diverting financial or managerial resources away from core priorities. This layered and geographically dispersed structure introduces heightened execution risks and complicates overall credit risk management.

### Limited Insider Buying Outside Major Shareholders Suggests Mixed Internal Confidence  
The relative lack of insider buying activity by executives apart from Berkshire Hathaway’s concentrated stake, coupled with observed insider share dispositions, may indicate emerging internal caution or differing views on the company’s medium-term outlook. This uneven confidence could undermine cohesion and effectiveness in implementing strategic initiatives vital to stabilizing financial metrics and credit quality, especially in a market environment requiring disciplined capital allocation.

### Minimal New Equity-Based Compensation Risks Governance Misalignment Over Time  
There was no significant issuance of stock-based compensation incentives in 2023. While this reduces near-term dilution risk, it may also reflect a compensation framework less aligned with shareholder value creation and disciplined capital management. In an industry frequently subject to price volatility and operational risk, a suboptimal alignment mechanism may impede management’s motivation to pursue strategies that balance growth, profitability, and credit risk mitigation over the long term.

---

## Operating Environment (F3)

### Elevated Geopolitical Risk from Russo-Ukrainian War Adds Market Volatility and Uncertainty  
The ongoing conflict in Eastern Europe amplifies volatility in global energy markets, disrupting supply chains and complicating commodity price forecasting. Occidental faces unpredictable operational challenges, including potential exposure to sanctions, shifting trade policies, and supply access constraints. This geopolitical instability complicates capital allocation and operational planning, increasing the risk profile through macroeconomic and market-instability channels.

### Heightened Regulatory and Transition Risks from Global Climate Initiatives  
Increasingly stringent global climate policies, spurred by COP28 outcomes and the Paris Agreement goals, place pressure on Occidental to reduce emissions and pivot toward lower-carbon activities. This regulatory tightening inflates compliance costs and raises the specter of asset stranding as fossil fuel demand evolves. The imperative to invest in sustainability or diversify portfolio mix imposes financial strain and strategic complexity, particularly amid capital expenditure pressures and margin erosion.

### Vulnerability to Natural Disasters and Environmental Incidents Risks Operational Disruption  
Occidental’s asset base remains exposed to severe weather and environmental hazards — ranging from hurricanes to oil spill liabilities — which historically have triggered costly production outages and remediation efforts. Climate-change–induced increases in the frequency and intensity of such events exacerbate this risk, potentially causing sudden operational and financial impacts that stress liquidity and credit risk metrics.

### Domestic Political and Social Unrest May Disrupt Operations and Increase Costs  
Ongoing episodes of social and political unrest in the U.S., including protests and civil tensions, elevate operational risks by raising security costs and potentially disrupting local activities. Such societal instability, while episodic, presents a persistent background risk factor that complicates long-term planning and risk mitigation, particularly in high-profile or community-sensitive jurisdictions.

---

## Conclusion

Occidental Petroleum’s 2023 credit risk profile is chiefly characterized by growing financial pressures borne from shrinking profitability, escalating capital expenditure, and rising leverage, which collectively compress financial flexibility and heighten refinancing vulnerabilities. The complexity of its subsidiary structure and concentrated ownership by Berkshire Hathaway introduce governance and strategic execution uncertainties, while limited insider confidence beyond dominant investors raises questions about internal alignment. Overlaying these firm-specific challenges is an external operating environment fraught with geopolitical volatility, intensifying regulatory constraints related to climate policy, and susceptibility to natural disasters and social unrest. Together, these factors elevate Occidental’s credit risk, underscoring the need for cautious financial management, strategic clarity, and proactive engagement with evolving market and regulatory dynamics to preserve creditworthiness going forward.

### Key Rating Drivers for Occidental Petroleum Corp – 2023

The credit risk profile of Occidental Petroleum in 2023 is shaped predominantly by a challenging financial performance marked by margin erosion and rising leverage, a complex and concentrated business structure, and an increasingly volatile external operating environment. While the company continues to generate strong absolute earnings, the interplay of internal and external pressures raises notable risks to credit stability, financial flexibility, and strategic execution.

---

## Financial Profile (F1)

**Erosion of Profitability and EBITDA Margin Compression**  
Occidental Petroleum’s EBITDA margin contracted sharply from 60.3% in 2022 to approximately 50.4% in 2023, signaling weakening profitability amid a difficult market backdrop. This decline reduces the company’s earnings cushion and intensifies pressure on cash generation, limiting the ability to absorb shocks or support aggressive debt reduction. When benchmarked against sector peers such as Diamondback Energy—which posted a much higher EBITDA margin near 74%—Occidental’s relative profitability deficit underscores structural competitive pressures and operational challenges in sustaining earnings quality.

**Deteriorating Free Cash Flow Conversion Amid Elevated Capital Spending**  
Although the company recorded strong EBITDA of $14.2 billion, its effectiveness in converting earnings into free cash flow deteriorated significantly, with the free cash flow ratio falling to 0.29 in 2023 from 0.59 in 2022. This decline was driven primarily by a surge in capital expenditures to $6.27 billion, nearly 40% higher than the prior year. The elevated investment intensity considerably diminished cash available after sustaining operations, compressing financial flexibility and intensifying refinancing risk in an environment of rising short-term liabilities and tighter liquidity.

**Rising Leverage and Moderately Compressed Coverage Metrics**  
Leverage ratios worsened moderately during 2023, with EBITDA leverage increasing to about 1.47x and funds from operations (FFO) leverage to 1.36x. While these leverage levels remain within moderate ranges, the trend is upward and reflects diminished cushioning relative to preexisting debt levels. Comparatively, peers like Diamondback Energy maintain substantially lower leverage below 1.0x FFO, which highlights Occidental’s relatively weaker capital structure. The company’s FFO interest coverage held at a comfortable 6.26x; however, this ratio is highly sensitive to earnings volatility given margin compression and increased debt, raising the risk of coverage deterioration under adverse cash flow scenarios.

**Liquidity Contraction and Increased Short-Term Refinancing Risk**  
Occidental’s liquidity ratio (cash to short-term debt) tightened sharply to approximately 1.35x in 2023 from a much stronger position in 2022. Despite a mild increase in absolute cash balances, the substantial growth in short-term liabilities curtailed near-term financial flexibility. Coupled with reduced free cash flow generation, this raises the risk that the company may face refinancing challenges or liquidity strain in volatile credit markets or during downturns in operating cash flows.

**Volatile Cash Flow Quality Amid Strong EBITDA**  
A noteworthy risk lies in substantial volatility across cash flow metrics. While EBITDA and FFO remain solid in absolute terms, the divergence between earnings and free cash flow growth underscores sensitivity to capital spending decisions. The decline in free cash flow despite stable operating cash suggests challenges in cash flow predictability and operational discipline, which could complicate effective financial planning and heighten liquidity risk in periods of market stress.

---

## Business Profile (F2)

**Concentrated Ownership Structure with Significant Berkshire Hathaway Influence**  
Berkshire Hathaway Inc. materially increased its shareholdings in Occidental in 2023, resulting in a dominant ownership position that centralizes strategic decision-making influence. Although this concentration can foster stability, it also risks reducing governance transparency and limiting the diversity of strategic perspectives necessary in a capital-intensive, cyclical energy sector. The company’s reliance on a principal institutional investor carries the risk of strategic shifts that may prioritize shareholder interests that do not fully align with minimizing credit risk.

**Complex and Diversified Subsidiary Network Heightens Operational and Strategic Risk**  
Occidental’s layered structure, incorporating major stakes in multiple affiliates such as Premcor Inc., California Resources Corp, Plains GP Holdings, NET Power Inc., and others, adds complexity to governance, capital allocation, and operational oversight. This breadth imposes risks that suboptimal performance or financial stress in affiliates could drain resources or distract management focus, potentially exacerbating volatility and weakening consolidated credit metrics.

**Limited Insider Buying Beyond Major Shareholders Signals Mixed Internal Confidence**  
Insider transaction activity beyond Berkshire Hathaway was muted, with limited purchases and some insider share dispositions in 2023. This uneven level of internal confidence may reflect cautiousness regarding the company’s medium-term outlook or strategic trajectory. Lack of widespread management buy-in may detract from cohesive execution of strategic initiatives critical to preserving financial strength and sustaining creditworthiness.

**Sparse Stock-Based Equity Compensation Could Impede Alignment**  
The absence of significant stock-based compensation transactions during the year mitigates near-term dilution but could suggest relatively weak incentives aligned with shareholder value creation. In a sector exposed to operational volatility, this may undermine management motivation for disciplined capital allocation and operational efficiencies, indirectly elevating long-term credit risk.

---

## Operating Environment (F3)

**Heightened Geopolitical Risks from Russo-Ukrainian Conflict**  
The prolonged Russo-Ukrainian war continues to destabilize global energy markets, introducing acute geopolitical risk to Occidental’s operating environment. Volatile commodity prices, supply chain disruptions, and the possibility of sanctions or trade limitations increase operational uncertainty and can constrain capital investment decisions. This hostile external landscape limits the company’s strategic flexibility and amplifies credit risk through market unpredictability.

**Escalating Regulatory Burdens Due to Climate Change Initiatives**  
Global climate policy evolution, accelerated by recent COP28 commitments and ongoing international accords, imposes stricter emissions controls and production constraints on fossil fuel companies like Occidental. The resultant compliance costs, potential asset stranding, and demand risks necessitate heightened capital deployment towards sustainability initiatives. These regulatory pressures could compress margins further and challenge long-term cash flow stability, intensifying credit concerns associated with the transition to low-carbon energy sources.

**Exposure to Domestic Political and Social Unrest Creates Operational and Reputational Risks**  
Operating primarily in the United States, Occidental remains exposed to episodic social and political unrest, including protests related to systemic injustice and election-related instability witnessed since 2020. These events heighten operational disruption risks, increase security costs, and complicate regulatory environments, which cumulatively inhibit stable operations and elevate risk premia in credit valuation.

**Vulnerability to Natural Disasters and Environmental Incidents**  
Historical experience with severe weather events such as Hurricanes Katrina and Ian and previous environmental incidents demonstrate Occidental’s exposure to asset damage, production interruptions, and costly remediation obligations. Climate change-linked increases in the frequency and severity of natural disasters exacerbate these risks, challenging operational continuity and placing additional strain on financial resources.

---

## Conclusion

Occidental Petroleum’s 2023 credit profile is marked by intensifying risk drivers across financial, business, and operating environment dimensions. Margin deterioration and free cash flow compression, compounded by rising leverage and liquidity tightening, weaken the company’s financial resilience. The business’s complex ownership and organizational structure further challenge governance effectiveness and strategic flexibility, while heavy reliance on a dominant institutional investor concentrates control and heightens governance risk. Externally, geopolitical tensions, accelerating climate regulations, domestic social unrest, and increasing environmental threats add layers of operational and market volatility.

Together, these factors underscore the need for disciplined capital allocation, proactive risk management, and enhanced financial flexibility to preserve credit quality. Occidental’s ability to navigate a highly uncertain macro and industry environment will be critical to maintaining its credit standing amid these multifaceted pressures.

### F1 – Elevated Leverage and Volatility in Cash Flow Generation  
Occidental Petroleum’s credit profile is constrained by its elevated debt burden, driven partly by its sizable capital expenditure program and legacy obligations from major acquisitions. Despite improvements in oil and gas prices during the forecast period, cash flow remains volatile due to exposure to commodity price fluctuations. The company’s leverage metrics, including net debt-to-EBITDA, remain at the higher end of the industry spectrum, limiting financial flexibility. ---

### F1 – Moderate Profitability under Pressure from Rising Costs  
Although Occidental benefits from cyclical oil price recoveries, profitability margins remain under pressure from elevated operating and capital costs, including increased expenditures on environmental compliance and asset maintenance. Rising inflationary pressures on labor, equipment, and energy inputs are expected to persist, weighing on operating margins and free cash flow generation. Reduced profitability tension constrains the company’s capacity to deleverage meaningfully over the near term and maintain robust coverage ratios.

---

### F2 – Concentrated Asset Base and Execution Risks from Transition Strategy  
Occidental’s strategic focus remains largely concentrated on upstream oil and gas production with limited diversification. Its recent strategic pivots towards enhanced oil recovery and carbon capture utilization and storage (CCUS) technologies impose execution risks, given the high capital intensity and uncertainties around regulatory incentives and commercialization timelines. Furthermore, governance challenges related to stakeholder pressures on sustainability initiatives may hamper strategic agility.

The operating environment is characterized by intensifying regulatory scrutiny and evolving environmental policies, particularly related to greenhouse gas emissions. Occidental faces risks from potential carbon pricing, stricter emissions regulations, and evolving investor expectations for ESG compliance, which could increase compliance costs and curtail operational flexibility. Additionally, the global energy transition is expected to reduce long-term demand for fossil fuels, raising uncertainty over asset valuations and creating strategic challenges to maintaining cash flow resilience.

---

### F3 – Macroeconomic and Market Risks from Oil Price Cyclicality  
Occidental’s credit risk is significantly influenced by macroeconomic factors, notably the cyclicality of oil and gas prices linked to global economic growth, geopolitical tensions, and OPEC+ production decisions. Periods of depressed commodity prices could severely constrain cash flow and liquidity, especially given the company’s leverage profile. Volatility in international trade and energy markets adds further unpredictability to revenue streams, exacerbating refinancing and investment risks in uncertain macro environments.

---

Overall, while Occidental Petroleum maintains a sizeable asset base and operational expertise, its credit profile is principally challenged by elevated leverage, volatility in profitability, strategic execution risks in a transitioning energy landscape, and an uncertain regulatory and macroeconomic environment. These factors collectively temper creditworthiness and underline the key credit risks for 2025.

### Key Rating Drivers – Financial Profile (F1)

**1. Declining Profitability and EBITDA Margin Pressure**  
Occidental Petroleum has experienced a notable contraction in its EBITDA margin from 60.3% in 2022 to 47.2% in 2024. This downward trend signals weakening profitability, which constrains the cushion available to absorb operational volatility and service debt obligations comfortably. The reduced margin also compares unfavorably to Diamondback Energy’s consistently higher EBITDA margins exceeding 69% during the same period, highlighting Occidental’s relative profitability challenges within the peer group. This erosion in operational profitability creates increased credit risk by compressing earnings buffers needed for deleveraging and capital investment.

**2. Erosion in Cash Flow Conversion and Free Cash Flow Generation**  
Although cash flow from operations (CFO) has remained positive, Occidental’s free cash flow (FCF) generation has declined sharply, falling from $12.3 billion in 2022 to approximately $4.4 billion in 2024. This deterioration largely reflects substantial capital expenditure growth, which surged from $4.5 billion in 2022 to over $7 billion in 2024, outpacing EBITDA growth. The diminishing free cash flow ratio, which dropped to just 16% in 2024 from nearly 59% in 2022, raises concerns regarding the company’s ability to generate sufficient internally sourced cash to fund capex and service debt. In comparison, competitor Diamondback’s FCF ratio also weakened but turned negative in 2024, underscoring sector-wide capex pressures but highlighting Occidental’s exposure given its larger scale and fixed commitments.

**3. Moderated Interest Coverage with Limited Headroom**  
Although Occidental’s funds from operations (FFO) interest coverage ratio remains above 5x, it declined from approximately 6.9x in 2022 to 5.3x in 2024. This reduction indicates dwindling capacity to absorb a drop in earnings without impairing interest payment ability. Under moderate stress scenarios involving a 10-20% fall in FFO, coverage ratios would compress further toward borderline levels, elevating refinancing and credit risk. The margin of safety is thinner compared to Diamondback’s 4.4x coverage ratio, though Occidental’s stronger absolute coverage is offset by less robust profitability trends.

**4. Elevated and Increasing Leverage Ratios Amid Earnings Declines**  
Leverage metrics have grown meaningfully over the past two years, with FFO leverage rising from 1.15x in 2022 to 1.92x in 2024, and EBITDA leverage increasing to over 2.1x in 2024. This trend reflects a combination of slower growth in operating cash flows relative to debt levels. While these leverage multiples remain moderate on an absolute basis, the accelerating trajectory amid declining profitability and cash flow generation may limit flexibility to elevate debt further without negative rating implications. By contrast, Diamondback’s FFO leverage worsened only modestly and remains below Occidental’s, giving competitors relatively more headroom.

**5. Liquidity Profile Shows Near-Term Strains Under Stress Scenarios**  
Occidental maintains a reasonable cash balance exceeding $2.1 billion as of 2024-end, alongside a cash-to-short-term debt ratio above 2.1x, signaling adequate immediate liquidity under stable conditions. However, the concurrently low free cash flow ratio and significant near-term capex obligations amplify vulnerability to liquidity pressure if cash flows weaken. Stress on liquidity could arise if cash flow declines materialize, as smaller operating cash flows paired with high capex commitments would disproportionately strain resources available to cover short-term liabilities. The liquidity risk is accentuated given the cyclical nature of the oil and gas sector and commodity price sensitivity.

**6. High Capital Expenditure Burden Limits Financial Flexibility**  
Occidental’s increasing capital expenditure requirements, which have risen by over 50% from 2022 to 2024, heighten leverage on operational cash flows and limit flexibility to allocate funds toward debt reduction or liquidity buffers. Sustained elevated capex spending at this scale constrains free cash flow generation and raises refinancing risk, especially if market conditions deteriorate or cash flow conversion weakens further. The growing capital intensity relative to EBITDA intensifies financial rigidity, complicating balance sheet management amidst an already challenged profitability environment.

---

**Summary**: The financial profile of Occidental Petroleum reflects a combination of eroding profitability margins, declining free cash flow generation, heightened leverage, and increasing capital intensity that collectively weigh on credit quality. While coverage ratios and liquidity remain adequate currently, ongoing margin pressure and cash flow constraints limit headroom and heighten refinancing and liquidity risks, particularly under adverse macroeconomic or commodity price shocks. These factors underscore a cautious credit outlook for the 2025 period.
